K-L1′ signed with two amendments (frozen leaf parameters from a real leaf; free-slip run also catches leaf-inertia drift). K-L2 signed. Enters the top five at #3; Shoal Bias to reserve. See notes/dialogue-A-turn19.md.

Drying Prints

  • Experience: You leave wet footprints on hot stone that shrink as they dry, then read a stranger's trail by its shrinking.
  • Origin: Nacre round 4; Breath Figure's record moved into daylight.
  • Nearest cliché: fading-trail drawing toys. It escapes because the fade is a calibrated clock you read in minute two.
  • Nearest past work: 0004 Breath Figure (water on a surface remembers). Differs: daylight, a reading task, someone else's trail. Also close to Pawl's Seriation.
  • Form: puzzle
  • Kill (pre-look): adjacent steps differ by about 0.2% of drying time, so step-to-step reading is expected to fail. It's dead unless a declared trail length shows a visible end-to-end gradient at 390 px and pauses can be found.

Which Way the Bicycle

  • Experience: You ride a bicycle over wet sand, then read a stranger's two tracks and find which way they went by laying a straightedge along the rear track.
  • Origin: Pawl round 5; a known track puzzle; Seriation's record-reading.
  • Nearest cliché: a geometry-puzzle app. It escapes because you make the evidence first, by riding.
  • Nearest past work: none in the archive; in this project, Seriation (read a record). Differs: the rule is geometric and exact, not statistical.
  • Form: puzzle
  • Kill (pre-look): dead if the wrong-direction tangent misses the front track by < 12 px on more than half of probes over the declared curvature range.

Frost Hollow

  • Experience: You plant hedges on a still evening, and the morning shows frost pooled behind them; then you cut a gap to drain the cold from one plant.
  • Origin: Pawl round 5; Waterline's dam, inverted to cold air.
  • Nearest cliché: garden or flow sandbox. It escapes because the night is skipped: place, then read the morning.
  • Nearest past work: 0004 Breath Figure (weather on a surface). Differs: daylight, a planning verb, terrain.
  • Form: puzzle
  • Kill (pre-look): dead if no-hedge vs one-hedge frost extent differs by < one hedge length at phone scale.

Catch Ruler

  • Experience: You catch a dropped ruler whose marks are in milliseconds, crowding as they rise, and learn how much of your reaction was waiting.
  • Origin: Pawl round 5; the schoolroom reaction test.
  • Nearest cliché: reaction-time test sites. Escape is thin: only the squared ruler.
  • Nearest past work: none yet.
  • Form: instrument
  • Kill (pre-look): dead if device input latency is comparable to what we show. Probably fires.

Three Rinses

  • Experience: You rinse a dyed bowl in one pour or several splashes and see splitting win; then the last jug is scarce.
  • Origin: Pawl round 5; washing up.
  • Nearest cliché: a chemistry-lesson slider.
  • Nearest past work: 0004 Breath Figure (conserved quantity on a surface). Differs: closed form, no sim.
  • Form: toy
  • Kill (pre-look): not failable as written (closed form, always large); dead unless minute two has a real trade. Weakest.

Dry Halo

  • Experience: You step on wet sand and the ring around your foot goes pale and dry; lift it and the print floods, because shearing packed grains pulls water in, not out.
  • Origin: Nacre round 5 (ideas/A.md); Reynolds dilatancy; Breath Figure's film moved to daylight sand.
  • Nearest cliché: the "footprints in sand" mood piece. Escapes because the footprint does the opposite of what you expect, and dwell time is a verb with a measurable answer.
  • Nearest past work: 0004 Breath Figure (a thin water film that records contact). Differs: daylight, granular, and the surprise is the sign of the effect, not the memory.
  • Form: toy
  • Kill (pre-look): halo must differ by ≥ 24 px or one tone step between a 0.5 s and a 5 s stand under sourced permeability; else "tap" is the only verb, dead.

Wake Angle

  • Experience: You speed a boat up across a lake and the wake's V keeps its 19.5° angle, then past a certain speed it starts to close.
  • Origin: Nacre round 5; Kelvin wake, and its narrowing at high Froude number (Rabaud & Moisy, from memory).
  • Nearest cliché: the ocean/water shader. Escapes with one flat grey lake and one wake; the angle is the subject, not the water.
  • Nearest past work: 0001 Phantom Jam (a pattern that travels behind a moving thing). Differs: a geometric constant that holds and then breaks, under one throttle verb.
  • Form: toy
  • Kill (pre-look): the cusp must come from a wave sum (≤ 64 components, 256² grid) at 30 fps on a mid phone; if we have to draw the V by hand, dead.

Railings

  • Experience: You walk past two railings, one behind the other, and a moiré band races along them faster than you walk; then you use its speed to tell how deep each garden is.
  • Origin: Nacre round 5; street noticing; records that carry information.
  • Nearest cliché: generative moiré patterns. Escapes because the moiré is a rangefinder for a real street, and you read it to answer a question.
  • Nearest past work: 0002 Plain Hunt (reading a pattern that rotates through positions). Differs: spatial, walking verb, the pattern is a measurement.
  • Form: tool
  • Kill (pre-look): dead if near bars need < 3 css px pitch at 390 px to give ≥ 2 visible bands, or if screen aliasing makes a false beat a cold reader can't separate.

Snow Roofs

  • Experience: You look down a snowy street where bare roofs mark leaking lofts, and insulate one to watch its snow stay.
  • Origin: Nacre round 5; a thermal map anyone can read.
  • Nearest cliché: the energy-saving explainer. Barely escapes; it's close to a lecture.
  • Nearest past work: 0004 Breath Figure (heat made visible by water). Differs: daylight, a street, roofs as a record.
  • Form: tool
  • Kill (pre-look): dead unless the visitor's act is the morning itself (drag the sun); a toggle plus an event on its own clock is lesson 4. Expected to die.
  • Nacre, turn 13, on Even Hours (notes/dialogue-A-turn13.md §3): by hand, the best frustum's drain-rate spread is about ±5% over the top ¾ of the pot but about ±20% when drained to 5% depth. The kill result is set entirely by how far down the marks go, so that depth x0 has to come from an object before anyone computes anything.

Three pitches, including the roads not taken

Pitch: Sounding

(Draft: Nacre, SHORTLIST round 2, turn 1. This replaces the round-1 Rubbing pitch, which is in git history and banked in notes/critique-B.md §5. Frozen rules: notes/top6-kills.md §1 and turns 8–10.)

Lone-visitor test, first: one hand raising one card is the whole input, so a single visitor moves everything. There is no crowd and no clock. Cheapest grey kill: a node geometry script over the declared gap set. K-S1″ is below.

The experience (second person, at most 150 words)

You stand in summer shade holding a white card. The ground around you is freckled with round suns, and your card catches a handful of them. Every coin is the same round sun, whatever hole in the leaves it came through. You lift the card toward the bough. All the coins shrink together. Then one of them stops being round: it turns into a sliver, a notch, the actual shape of the gap above you. You can't see the canopy, because you never look up. The card is your only instrument and every lift is a sounding. Some gaps resolve at your waist and some at full stretch. Most never do: they stay suns, because your arm ends before the light does. You leave with a chart of soundings, a few shapes, and many coins that keep their secret.

The first 30 seconds

The ground dapple is the default view. It contains the card's own hard-edged shadow, which says "sun" with no label. The card follows your pointer. Dragging up raises it, and every coin on it visibly shrinks together, in time with your hand. That uniform shrink is the readable event. By about 20 seconds a near gap resolves at mid-height: one coin goes sharp-edged and odd-shaped. Nothing is written on screen.

The five-minute arc

  • 0–1 min: lift and lower. Coin size follows the card's height, so you learn that the card measures distance.
  • 1–3 min: hunt for shapes. Each resolve happens at a different height. A gap of width w at height h resolves when w ≥ (h−c)/108, where c is the card height and 1/108 rad is the sun's diameter. One metre of arm moves the resolve line by 9.26 mm of gap width. You learn which coins are worth chasing.
  • 3–5 min: the limit. At the 2.0 m reach (sourced median) the remaining coins stay round. Your chart of soundings (heights where something resolved) is the only record, and it ends on the coins you could not read. There is no reveal and no tree.

Why this hasn't been done this way (nearest existing works, with links)

  • The pinhole/sun-coin classroom demo (pinhole camera; eclipse crescents under trees). There, the round coin is the lesson. Here, the round coin is the thing you fail to read, and the card's height is the verb.
  • Our own Under the Tree (this project, banked) showed big gaps as blobs and small ones as coins, but it had no verb. Sounding gives the visitor the axis that matters: distance.
  • We know of no interactive piece where the visitor's reach is the limit of what can be known. We have not searched exhaustively.

Form, mood and main material

A ritual and an instrument. Quiet summer daylight, the feeling of standing still under a tree at noon. The materials are warm light on paper, leaf-shadow greens on grass, and one white A6 card. Silent.

Technical approach (at most 120 words)

The canopy is a hidden set of gaps (polygons) at 3–4 m. Each gap's image on the card is its shape convolved with the sun disc of radius (h−c)/216 m. A fragment shader draws it from a per-gap blur precomputed at a few radii. Coins are additive on the card and on the ground dapple. The card is a quad following the pointer: x is position, y is height. The soundings chart is a strip at the card's edge. Seeded gap generator: widths are log-uniform 3–30 mm [declared before any data], openness is 5%. A node script computes the resolve classes. The reduced-motion picture is a still card with a step slider.

Biggest risk, and the sketch that would test it

Risk: the work is true but empty. If almost no gap resolves within reach, or almost all already resolve at the waist, the lift does nothing. K-S1″ (signed): dead if the lift band is smaller than the class already resolved at 1 m. By Pawl's proof it passes by construction for any top width under 37 mm, so with the declared 3–30 mm range it is not a test. The helper budget is spent, so no width source is coming (h-0005-5 found none). The pitch states: K-S1″ untested. The sketch that can fail: a grey 390×844 capture of the card at 1.0, 1.5 and 2.0 m on 3 seeds. Framing frozen first: the card's 105 mm side spans 0.9 × 390 px. The 1→2 m coin shrink (9.26 mm at every h) is then about 31 px, stated as a consequence, not a test. K-S2 (signed both, ⅓ [chosen] and fixed before any run): over 3 seeds × 50 card positions at 2.0 m, drawn uniformly over the dapple by the script (not placed by us), at least ⅓ of positions must hold a shape that is resolved at 2.0 m, not resolved at 1.0 m, and ≥ 24 px. Fewer than ⅓: dead.

What would make it fail

  • The grey capture kill above.
  • K-S-cold: a review helper given only the capture sequence can't say "lifting the card changes what the light shows". The ground-dapple cue is default, not a rescue.
  • It reads as a pretty dapple screensaver: the bloom-and-dapple cliché. The defence is that light is never decorative here; every coin is data.
  • Coin density is unsourced. Overlapping coins on an A6 card (about 45 fit at c = 1 m) could make shapes illegible.

Connection to earlier works (optional)

0004 Breath Figure: light on a surface carrying information. This time it is in daylight (intention 3), and the record is soundings, not a trace.

CRITIQUE revision (Nacre, CRITIQUE turn 3; the one revision allowed, answering novelty-5)

  • Record = a knotted string. The soundings chart is replaced by a string hanging from the card's edge. You tie a knot at each height where a coin resolved (after Minnaert's habit of measuring with a string). The record is a physical object, and it carries information: knot spacing equals the resolve heights.
  • A fixed date and hour, stated in the about panel. The sun is not overhead, so ground coins are ellipses, elongated by 1/sin(elevation). The card is held level, so card coins are ellipses too until you tilt it. A shape that keeps its outline when the ellipse is gone is the true first cue: "this one is the gap, not the sun".
  • Citations to add: M. Minnaert, Light and Colour in the Open Air (sun images under trees), and komorebi as the everyday name. No photo-traced tree: we have no licensed photo, and the gap set stays procedural.
  • Honesty about the stake: "your reach is the limit" is our chosen 2.0 m ceiling (sourced median reach, but chosen by us). No skill moves it. The pitch says so in plain words, and the work's about panel says "the ceiling is ours".
  • Pass scope: K-S2 passed (88/150 = 58.7%) on our declared density and widths only. Halving density gives about 36%, close to the ⅓ line. K-S1″ is untested. K-S-cold and the grey card capture are unrun and still owed before DECIDE.

Pitch: Spoon and Saucer

(Draft: Nacre, SHORTLIST round 2, turn 1. Pawl's stranger version from turn 18. This replaces the round-1 pitch, which is in git history. Kills: notes/dialogue-B-turn18.md §2, signed in A-turn19 and A-turn21.)

Lone-visitor test, first: one pointer, two places to put it (in the tea, or on the saucer rim). The flow answers hand position over time. There is no crowd and no clock of its own. Cheapest grey kill: an axisymmetric (r, z) node run at hand Ω = 5, 10, 15, 20 rad/s with a frozen leaf. K-L0 and K-L1′ are below.

The experience (second person, at most 150 words)

You look down into a white cup of tea on a saucer, in morning light. Loose leaves lie on the bottom. You stir with a spoon and the tea swirls. When you stop, the leaves on the bottom don't fly outward as you'd expect. They creep inward and pile into a small heap in the middle. Then you try the other thing: you leave the spoon out and turn the cup itself on its saucer. The tea hardly moves at first, but the leaves on the bottom slide outward into a ring against the wall. Same leaves, same cup, opposite answers. Soon you are playing it: turn to make a ring, stir to make a heap, stop at the right moment. You learn a rule you didn't have words for: leaves go where the bottom is slower than the water.

The first 30 seconds

The cup is still and seen top-down, with leaves scattered on the bottom. A circling drag inside the cup stirs it, and the surface shows the swirl through drifting floaters and a faint dimple. On release the heap forms. K-L0 requires the bottom leaves' median radius to fall by R/3 within 10 s at the slowest hand Ω. That one surprise ("they went in?") is the 30-second event. The saucer rim is visible as a second, grippable ring. Nothing is written on screen.

The five-minute arc

  • 0–1 min: stir and stop, and the heap forms. Floating leaves go outward while bottom leaves go inward: two classes moving in opposite directions.
  • 1–3 min: turn the cup. From rest, the bottom layer is dragged faster than the interior (spin-up), so leaves go out to the ring. The minute-one rule breaks and a better rule replaces it.
  • 3–5 min: the skill. Gather every leaf into one ring, then into one heap, by timing a turn and a stop. Spin-up and spin-down e-fold in roughly H/√(νΩ): about 27 s at Ω=5 and 13 s at Ω=20 for H=6 cm (hand estimate, ν of water, unsourced form), so the stops matter. The cup keeps whatever arrangement you leave. An optional coda: the same law cuts a river bend (Einstein 1926), shown only if it costs no clarity.

Why this hasn't been done this way (nearest existing works, with links)

  • Tea leaf paradox: demonstrated in videos and lab demos, all of which show the stir only. We know of none that hands the visitor the reverse (turning the cup) as a second verb, or makes ring-then-heap a skill.
  • Fluid-sim toys (stir a pretty liquid): the cliché. This escapes it because there is one paradox, two particle classes, a plain top-down view, and a goal.
  • Searched informally only.

Form, mood and main material

A toy with a skill in it, close to a game without a score. Domestic, unhurried morning daylight: a white cup, amber tea, dark leaves, a saucer's glaze. Silent, or the faint spoon-on-china only if it can be checked numerically. Default is silence.

Technical approach (at most 120 words)

Axisymmetric swirl, ν and Ω only, on an (r, z) grid. The bottom has no slip and the wall turns with the saucer when the cup is turned. With 0.08 mm cells near the floor to resolve the Ekman layer, the explicit step is about 6.4 ms, so 2–3 substeps per frame (estimated, not measured). Leaves are about 200 particles with a frozen density and size (proxy 1.05–1.15 g/cm³, 1–3 mm [chosen]; no helper budget left to source them) and a bottom-friction rule frozen before any run. The stir is a body force from the drag path. The render is a top-down cup quad with refraction, leaves as sprites, and the surface dimple from the swirl profile. Reduced motion slows playback, keeping the same physics.

Biggest risk, and the sketch that would test it

Risk: the paradox comes from our leaf model, not the flow. A dense leaf drifts outward under centrifugal force in any swirl. Or the effect is too slow for a hand. Sketch (grey, node, then one 390×844 capture):

  • K-L1′: with no slip, ≥ 80% of bottom leaves end inside R/3 at every Ω in 5–20 rad/s. A free-slip control run must not do this, which proves the mechanism is the Ekman flux.
  • K-L2: turning a still cup from rest for 10 s puts ≥ 60% of bottom leaves outside 2R/3 [signed].
  • K-L0: see the first 30 seconds above.

What would make it fail

  • Fails K-L1′ or K-L2, or passes only with a leaf tuned after a result was seen.
  • K-L0 cuts (signed both, frozen before any run; metric = bottom-leaf median radius falls by R/3): at Ω=5, ≤ 10 s passes, 10–20 s drops it to the bottom of the order, > 20 s kills it. It must also reach ≤ 10 s at every Ω ≥ 10 or it drops to the bottom.
  • A thumb can't hold a circling drag at 1–3 rev/s for 10 s on a phone. The fallback is to run at about 6 rad/s.
  • Solver cost exceeds a frame on a mid phone (unmeasured).
  • It reads as a fluid toy and nobody tries the saucer. The saucer rim must invite a grip on its own.

Connection to earlier works (optional)

0004 Breath Figure (a physical film simulation under one light). This one is in daylight, with flow instead of condensation and two verbs instead of breath.

Pitch: Seriation

(Draft: Pawl, SHORTLIST round 2, turns 2 and 4. It replaces Nacre's Second Bend placeholder, which is in git history. Second Bend goes to reserve; the reason is in notes/shortlist2-B-turn2.md §1. Frozen rules: notes/top6-kills.md §6, Nacre's turn-9 signatures (K-Q1′–Q4), and turn 4's A1/A2/K-Q5 (notes/shortlist2-B-turn4.md). The objective, generator and display are frozen below, before any run.)

Lone-visitor test, first: one finger drags one row. There's no crowd, no clock and no physics, so nothing moves unless the visitor moves it. Nothing on the board has a value we tuned by hand, only declared ranges (below). Cheapest grey kill: a node script over 200 seeds. It needs no source, no shader and no browser. K-Q1′–Q3 and K-Q5 are all decided by it.

The experience (second person, at most 150 words)

You sit at a store-room table with twelve grave lots from one cemetery that nobody dated. Each lot is a row of pots in ink on buff card, and every pot type has its own column, where the pots of a kind stand nested like a shelf stack. You drag rows up and down. Gaps in a column look wrong, so you close them. As the gaps close, each type's stacks swell and then thin down its column, like a spindle. It is born, it is popular, it dies out. The order shows up in the pots. But it reads the same upside down. Time has no direction until you turn over the one lot that held a coin. Then one pot refuses: whatever order you try, it sits outside its own spindle, far below where its kind died out. Someone was buried with something old. You find which grave.

The first 30 seconds

Twelve shuffled rows fill a phone screen (12 × 44 px rows; silhouettes ≥ 24 px; at most 4 pots per cell, drawn as overlapping profiles offset 10 px, so a full stack is 54 px in a 65 px column). Every column is ragged. The first drag that puts two rows with shared types next to each other closes a gap in one column. That gap closing is the readable event: you can see it without being told the rule. Nothing is written on screen. The coin lot looks like the others until it is tapped.

The five-minute arc

  • 0–1 min: close gaps. The rule you learn is "a kind of pot lives in one unbroken stretch of time".
  • 1–3 min: the spindles. Stack heights swell and thin, so you start ordering by how many, not just whether. The good order turns out to have no top. Tapping the coin lot fixes which end is old, and the whole board flips if you had it backwards. (This minute exists only if K-Q5 passes. If it fails, the fallback is written now: cap at 2 per cell, cut this minute, and the arc goes 0–2 min gaps and the coin, 2–5 min the heirloom.)
  • 3–5 min: the heirloom. Every good order leaves the same one pot stranded. The minute-one rule ("every pot sits where its kind lived") breaks for one person, which is the point. Tapping that grave ends the puzzle with one plain line about why people keep old things. The board stays as you left it.

Why this hasn't been done this way (nearest existing works, with links)

  • Seriation (archaeology)): Petrie's sequence dating, Kendall's work on Münsingen, and battleship-curve charts in textbooks. Those show the finished chart. Here you build it, and the chart is the pots: stack height, never a bar.
  • Drag-to-sort puzzles and nonograms: the cliché is a spreadsheet with drawings. This escapes it only if the spindles read as pots, and if the last answer comes from an exception, not a completion.
  • We know of no interactive seriation with a planted heirloom. We searched informally only.

Form, mood and main material

A puzzle with no clock: our first. It has a quiet store-room mood in daylight, careful and a little elegiac, and it shows no remains, only inventories. The materials are ink silhouettes (lathe profiles) stacked on buff archival card, pencil gaps and one bronze coin. Silent.

Technical approach (at most 120 words)

No physics. A seeded generator draws declared ranges: 12 lots, 6 types, lifespans ¼–½ of the span, lot sizes 3–8, one heirloom ¼–½ span after its type ends, drawn like any lot with no rescue. Cells over 4 display as 4; scripts score displayed counts. Objective, frozen: minimise Σ(run length − occupied rows); ties broken by Σ over types of max(0, direction changes along the run − 1), lower wins. A node script finds the optimum by branch and bound. WebGL draws instanced SDF pot profiles with ink bleed on card, plus row-slide easing. Reduced motion means instant row swaps. Phone: 6 columns of 65 px at 390 px.

Biggest risk, and the sketch that would test it

Risk: the puzzle is filler, either too easy for a greedy rule or ambiguous, so the heirloom isn't a clean answer, or the optimiser buries it. Sketch (node, 200 seeds, frozen generator):

  • K-Q1′ trivial: dead if nearest-neighbour chaining finds the best order in ≥ ½ of seeds.
  • K-Q2 impossible: dead if the best order isn't unique up to reversal and adjacent swaps in ≥ ½ of seeds.
  • K-Q3 heirloom: dead if the heirloom isn't the unique single-type outlier of every best order in ≥ 80% of seeds. Diagnostic, recorded with no re-run: the share of K-Q3 fails in which the best order moved the heirloom lot next to its type's run.
  • K-Q5 spindle: the 1–3 min arc is cut (fallback above) if fewer than ½ of seeds have a type with ≥ 3 distinct nonzero displayed count levels. Diagnostic: share of cells clipped at 4.
  • K-Q4 phone: dead if a 390×844 grey capture can't show 12 rows × 6 columns with silhouettes ≥ 24 px and a 4-stack inside 65 px.

What would make it fail

  • Any of K-Q1′–Q4. A failed generator is not re-tuned. If the declared ranges fail, the pitch drops. K-Q5 failing cuts a minute, not the work.
  • The optimiser burying the heirloom: then the puzzle teaches the visitor to hide the one person it is about.
  • It reads as a spreadsheet: a cold reader of the grey capture describes "a table", not "pots changing over time".
  • WebGL is close to garnish here. If it adds nothing beyond ink on card, we say so plainly.
  • Graves: the tone must stay archival, never morbid.

Connection to earlier works (optional)

None in the archive. Within this project it is a sibling of Which Way the Bicycle (reading a record), and it is the only pitch whose answer is a person, not a law.

Sketches

1-under-the-tree

Findings: sketch 1, Under the Tree (K-T1)

The question this sketch tested

Is the card a focus knob with one sweet spot, or does the band of "round, separate, resolved-crescent" card heights move at least 2× (h_lo) before it closes, on a generated leaf mask at 2–20% openness, with no chosen value?

What we built, and what we faked

  • sim.mjs: leaf-gap mask on a 0.1 cm grid (open ellipses, lognormal sizes σ0.6, aspect 1–4, random angle) ⊗ lit part of the sun disc (diameter h/108) minus a moon disc of equal size at cover m. Image = sum over gap pixels of the kernel.
  • sweep.mjs: per gap, binarise the full-image patch at 0.5 × that gap's isolated peak and compute IoU against the ideal crescent. Score(h,m) = area-weighted share of gaps with IoU ≥ 0.7 (also 0.5). Band = score ≥ 0.5. 24 heights (12–400 cm) × 7 m × openness 2/5/10/20% × 3 seeds. Raw outputs: sweep-*.txt.
  • Faked: grey only; no eye adaptation (images dim as m rises); gap-size scale and gap count are NOT sourced; two mask modes tried (crowding: more gaps of fixed size, which merge into a lattice at ≥10%; fixed count: gaps grow with openness). Count 30/100/200 per 30×30 cm.
  • Not tested: pointer, sky, phone cost. The page index.html is only a render of heights × m.

What we saw (capture paths)

captures/keep/kt1-contact-sheet-5pct.png (also captures/2026-10-16_00-29-52/): 5% openness, 15 panels. At m=0 the spots go from gap-shaped (h=25) through round coins (110–170) to overlapping haze with the big gap still a bright blob (250). At m=0.5 and 0.8, small and mid gaps show clean crescents all bitten the same way at h=170–250, while the largest gaps stay blobs at every height and the whole image dims a lot at m=0.8. The crescents are real and lovely, but at 5% they appear only at 1.7–2.5 m for gaps of ~0.7 cm median.

What we learned

  • Strict kill (area-weighted ≥50% of light, IoU 0.7): fails. h_lo ratio: 2% openness 1.2–1.9 (window closes by m≈0.3–0.5); at ≥5% the 50% band never opens (best 33%, 5% at 10%, 8% at 20%). Lenient IoU 0.5: passes only at 2% (ratio 2.7, window to m=0.7), 1.4 at 5%, nothing at ≥10%.
  • Why: a spread of gap sizes plus slivers means the biggest gaps (which hold most of the light) never become round. There is no single band; the score is a population effect.
  • The picture is better than the number: as m rises the set of gaps that read as crescents shifts toward smaller gaps. In short, the chase is "which spots are crescents now", not "where is the sweet spot". That is Nacre's earlier prediction, and now seen.
  • Scale is a lever (median gap size, count); the ratio test is scale-free, so the failure is not a scale artefact. Reaching crescents needs h ≈ 108 × gap size / (1−m), so real mm-sized leaf gaps would sit well inside 2 m.
  • Dimming at high m is large; any version needs a physical exposure rule (eye adaptation) that we would be inventing.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

Less exciting as pitched (a moving window the visitor chases fails its own kill); more exciting as a different thing: every spot on the card has its own height at which it becomes a crescent, and the eclipse makes you hunt for the small ones while the big ones stay blobs. Not sketched: whether that reads for a visitor (K-T2). Recommend: Under the Tree stays alive only if the verb is reframed as "find the gaps small enough to show the moon" and a count-weighted, brightness-aware metric is pre-registered before more sweeps. Otherwise Rubbing (Pawl's K-R1/R2) leads and Capstan is next.

Addendum: K-T2 (reframed verb), Pawl, run exactly as frozen in notes/kt2-preregistration.md

Output: sketches/1-under-the-tree/kt2-result.txt; script notes/scripts/kt2.mjs. Numbers only, no capture. Primary (5%, count100): at m=0.8 only 4% of visible gaps read as crescents (needed 20%); median size ratio 0.70 (needed <=0.5). 2%: 13% / 0.73. 10%: 1% / 0.17. Size-mode 1 cm: share 1-3%. FAIL at every openness and in both mask modes. Reading: even choosing the best height, crescents at deep eclipse are rare, and the ones that read are not much smaller than the round-spot ones. "Find the small gaps" is not a distinct verb in this sim. Caveat for the record: IoU 0.7 on crescents at 0.1 cm grid may be harsh for tiny gaps; not changed after the fact. Under the Tree goes to reserve; no second re-test, as agreed.

2-rubbing

Findings: sketch 2, Rubbing (K-R1/K-R2)

The question this sketch tested

Does the best rubbing pressure differ by at least 2x between a crisp and a worn line, at every wear fraction f in 0.1-0.9, whichever way the paper bends (tension n=2, plate n=4, mixed), with T and D swept 0.3-3x? And is there a real trade-off (K-R1 clause 2)? Rules frozen in notes/kr-preregistration.md before the run.

What we built, and what we faked

kr.mjs (copy in notes/scripts/kr.mjs): 1D obstacle-contact solve (primal-dual active set, dense, dx 0.1 mm) of a sheet over the rounded-V profile. Crisp 3 mm deep x 6 mm wide; worn d=3(1-f), w=6(1+0.5f). Fill = share of groove width with gap <= 0.1 d. p* = smallest p with Fill >= 0.9. Faked or skipped: no render, no capture (numbers only), lichen coverage NOT run, lichen growth rate not used. Nothing visual to look at, so captures/keep has no new file.

What we saw (capture paths)

No capture. Numbers: sketches/2-rubbing/kr-result.txt. Ratio p(worn)/p(crisp):

  • f=0.1: 0.82 (n=2), 0.72 (n=4), 0.73-0.77 (mixed). Fails.
  • f=0.3: 0.53 (n=2), 0.40 (n=4), 0.42-0.48 (mixed). n=2 fails by 0.03.
  • f=0.5: 0.32 / 0.21 / 0.22-0.28. f=0.7: 0.17 / 0.09. f=0.9: 0.05 / 0.02. f=0.95: 0.02 / 0.01.
  • T, D scaling moves p* exactly in proportion and leaves every ratio unchanged (cancels, as derived). The mixed-model length (0.8-5 mm) moves the ratio by under 0.05.
  • K-R1 clause 2 passes at every f: crisp line Fill at the worn line's p* is 0.10-0.51 (light rubbing fails the crisp line), worn line Fill at the crisp line's p* is 1.00 (hard rubbing over-works the worn line). Real trade-off.

What we learned

Strict K-R2 FAILS as registered, at f=0.1 for every model and at f=0.3 for n=2. It fails there for a plain reason: a line worn by 10% is nearly the crisp line, so no pressure gap can exist. The gap grows smoothly with f and is large (3x to 40x) from f=0.5 up. The trade-off clause passes everywhere. So the mechanism is real and needs no paper constant, but it only bites for a line already half gone. The brief ("arrives pre-worn, common line nearly gone, obscure line crisp") sits in that range, which suggests the 0.1-0.9 range was drawn too wide on the low end. That is a post-hoc reading and I am not re-scoring on it. Caveats: Fill counts the trivial lip (about 20% of width has depth under 0.1 d), so p* is slightly optimistic for both lines alike; 1D profile only.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

Strict verdict: kill triggered, Rubbing drops and Capstan moves up (K-C1 next). Mechanism-wise more exciting than feared: a clean 3-40x gap with no tuned constant for f>=0.5. The decision whether the scope "f>=0.5 only" counts as a legitimate new question, not a rescue, is Nacre's call and then a joint one. Unrun: lichen coverage (30% rule), the cold read, any graphics.

3-capstan

Findings: sketch 3, Capstan (K-C1)

The question this sketch tested

Is Capstan a brake dial? i.e. does one wrap angle (per barge) dock everything, or does docking need a changing wrap? Pre-registered in notes/kc-preregistration.md before any run. The pitch's own kill ("a constant theta fails some speed") was trivially true across cases, so it was sharpened: open-loop vs closed-loop with barge load and mu hidden from the visitor.

What we built, and what we faked

Node sim (notes/scripts/kc.mjs, copy sketches/3-capstan/sim.mjs): 1D barge, F = T0 e^(mu theta), 81 hidden combos (v, gap, M, mu) x 200 trials, hand offset 6 deg, 0.2 s lag, rate limit 1 turn/s. Faked: no rendering, no phone drag, no heat, no static grip, no snap-back dynamics. Hidden M range (60-150 t) and break load (60 kN) are our assumptions, not sourced. The feedback policy C is my stand-in for a skilled hand, run once, untuned.

What we saw (capture paths)

captures/keep/kc1-dockrates.png (I looked at it): a grey bar chart of the K-C1 dock rates drawn from kc-result.txt by sketches/3-capstan/index.html, with the 90% pass line in red. It shows 11.3 / 11.9 / 75.5% for the hidden-load rows, with feedback below the line, and 96.9% for mu known. It is a chart of numbers, not a playable sketch. Numbers: sketches/3-capstan/kc-result.txt, post-hoc diag kc-diag-posthoc.txt. Not seen: any gameplay image or phone jog-wheel.

What we learned

Hidden M and mu: A (one theta) 11.3% dock; B (open-loop per case) 11.9%; C (feedback) 75.5%, parts 7.4%, theta moved >10 deg in 100% of docked runs. mu known: B 32.5%, C 96.9%. M known: B 32.5%, C 74.1%. Nothing hidden: B 97.5% (a brake dial), C 100%. So the surge is real: when anything is hidden, open-loop fails and feedback docks about 6x more often; mu alone does most of the damage to B. B only works when the visitor knows everything. Strict pass rule: C needed >=90% docks and <5% parts. It got 75.5% and 7.4%. STRICT FAIL on that clause (other clauses pass; the brake-dial kill did not trigger). Post-hoc diagnostic (not part of the test): all C failures sit in the short-gap, fast cases (gap 1 m with v>=0.5, plus 0.75 m/s at 2.5 m), where required force is 13-40 kN against a 60 kN break load with 0.2 s lag. The 7.4% parts are the first-contact wrap at mu=0.4, M=150 t, set from the nominal prior before any feedback acts. Slow and long cases dock 100%. This suggests the grid contains near-impossible barges and C has no "start light", but changing either now is a re-scope, not a rescue.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

More exciting than expected on the mechanism (closed-loop beats open-loop 6x, with no hand-set lever except the assumed ranges), less on the registered rule: strict FAIL. Capstan as pitched does not pass K-C1. Same standing as Rubbing: mechanism sound, strict rule failed, reserve only with a new registered question (start-light policy, feasible-case range stated first). Note the phone drag (jog-wheel) was never tested and is the other named risk.

4-seriation

Findings: sketch 4, Seriation (node script, no browser)

The question this sketch tested

Is the frozen Seriation generator a puzzle at all: not solved by greedy chaining (K-Q1′), not ambiguous (K-Q2), with the heirloom a clean answer in every best order (K-Q3), and does the spindle give ≥3 count levels (K-Q5)?

What we built, and what we faked

gen.js (seeded generator) and run.js (exact branch-and-bound over all 12! orders, collecting every optimum, ties by A2's direction-change rule). Run once on seeds 1–200 and not re-run. No capture exists: it is node only, and K-Q4 (phone grey capture) was not run. Interpretations I made where the pitch was silent [I], all marked in the code: spindle weight is a triangle over the lifespan; pots per lot are drawn with replacement by weight; a lot with no live type is rejected and redrawn (661 redraws); the heirloom pot replaces one random pot of its lot; K-Q2 "unique up to reversal and adjacent swaps" is read two ways (strict: one class mod reversal; connected: optima linked by adjacent swaps); K-Q3 strict means only the heirloom type has gaps, the rest of its run is contiguous, and the heirloom lot sits ≥1 row off the run, in every optimum. Pawl has not checked these yet.

What we saw (capture paths)

Numbers only: captures/keep/seriation-run-200.txt (per-seed rows in sketches/4-seriation/results.csv).

  • K-Q1′: greedy finds the full optimum in 12.5% (primary score only: 33.5%). Passes (not trivial).
  • K-Q2: 5.5% strict, 42.5% connected. Both under ½. Dead.
  • K-Q3: 7.5% strict (loose reading 20%) against the 80% bar. Dead. Of the 185 fails, 144 had a best order that moved the heirloom lot next to its type's run.
  • K-Q5: 200/200 have a type with ≥3 levels; 15.1% of cells clip at 4. Passes.
  • The best order itself has a mean of 2.47 gaps.

What we learned

  • The heirloom signal is 2–5 gaps. The generator's own sampling noise (a live type missing from a lot by chance) puts about 2.5 gaps into the best order anyway. The signal is the same size as the noise, so the optimiser prefers to bury the heirloom (A1's fear, now measured: 78% of fails).
  • Not a threshold miss: Q3 is 7.5% or 20% against 80%, so no reading of my [I] choices rescues it. By the frozen rule the generator is not re-tuned.
  • K-Q5 held, so the spindle idea is fine; the puzzle's answer is what fails.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

Less. The pitch's reward (one pot that refuses every order) is not produced by the declared ranges. Per the pitch, failing K-Q2/Q3 drops it. A changed form (the visitor chooses which grave is the odd one from a smaller, cleaner fixed set) would be a new pitch, not this one. Next in order: Sounding K-S2, then Spoon and Saucer K-L0.

5-sounding

Findings: sketch 5, Sounding (node script, no browser)

The question this sketch tested

K-S2 as frozen in pitches/1.md: over 3 seeds x 50 uniform card positions at 2.0 m, do at least 1/3 hold a gap resolved at 2.0 m, not resolved at 1.0 m, and >= 24 px?

What we built, and what we faked

ks2.js (Pawl, run once). Faked: no render. Interpretations [I], listed in the file header: Poisson gap centres, density 0.05/E[w^2], square-ish gaps, h uniform 3-4 m, width log-uniform 3-30 mm, image size = gap size, 3.34 px/mm from the frozen framing. The 24 px floor never binds (it is 7.2 mm; the newly resolved band is 13.9-23 mm).

What we saw (capture paths)

Numbers only: captures/keep/sounding-ks2.txt. Seeds 35/50, 27/50, 26/50; total 88/150 = 58.7% against 33.3%. PASS. My hand estimate before the run was about 59% (0.89 expected newly-resolved gaps per card, Poisson).

What we learned

  • The pass is real arithmetic but rests on two numbers we declared: 5% openness and log-uniform 3-30 mm. The newly resolved band is ln(23.1/13.9)/ln10 = 22% of gaps; a thinner canopy or a smaller top width lowers it fast. Halving density gives about 36%, near the line. Both are unsourced, so this is a pass of the geometry, not of the world.
  • K-S1'' and K-S2 now both pass for the same reason: our declared width range. Still untested: whether the lift reads on a phone (grey capture) and K-S-cold.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

Neutral-to-more: it survives its one runnable kill with margin, but the margin is ours. The pitch must say "passes on declared density and widths".

6-spoon

Findings: sketch 6, Spoon and Saucer (K-L0)

The question this sketch tested

K-L0: after a stir stops, does the bottom-leaf median radius fall by R/3 within 10 s at Ω=5 (≤10 pass, 10–20 bottom, >20 dead) and within 10 s at every Ω≥10?

What we built, and what we faked

Node only, kl0.js and kl0b.js. Analytic model chosen for checkability: the interior spins down over a still floor with dΩ/dt = −Ω√(νΩ)/H (Ekman suction), the Ekman layer gives u_r = −Ωr·e^−ζ sin ζ, δ=√(ν/Ω), and a leaf feels the mean over its thickness (0.5, 1, 2 mm). R=4 cm, H=6 cm, water ν. Faked: solid-body initial swirl (a real stir is not), no (r,z) numeric solve, no leaf size in the heap (leaves collapse to a point), no friction in kl0.js. kl0b.js adds a friction threshold only as a sensitivity sweep. The leaf thickness and threshold are [chosen]/unsourced.

What we saw (capture paths)

No capture; node output. captures/keep/spoon-kl0.txt and captures/keep/spoon-kl0b.txt.

  • t_pass at Ω=5: 0.5 s (0.5 mm leaf), 0.6 s (1 mm), 1.1 s (2 mm). At Ω=10–20: 0.1–0.8 s. Far inside the 10 s bar.
  • With a friction threshold, the result is a cliff, not a slope: unchanged up to 8 mm/s, then never (>60 s) between 12 and 20 mm/s. Initial leaf speeds are 2–4 cm/s.
  • Ekman Re = Vδ/ν = 89 (Ω=5) to 179 (Ω=20) at the wall. That is above the roughly 55 instability limit, so the real layer is not laminar and our formula is approximate at the edge.

What we learned

  • K-L0 passes, and by too much to be a test: the bar was set in seconds and the model answers in about a second. It does not fail at Ω=5, so the "27 s e-fold" in the pitch is the wrong clock for the heap. The e-fold governs the interior spin-down, not leaf transport.
  • The real risk moves. A collapse in about 1 s may be too fast to read as "they crept in", or may be what a stir really does with a threshold. What decides it is the leaf friction threshold (unsourced), where the model flips from 0.6 s to never at around 10–15 mm/s. A threshold at that level, which a real wet leaf could have, kills the paradox in our model. K-L0 as written cannot see this.
  • K-L1′ (free-slip control) and K-L2 (turn the cup) are not run. Both need the (r,z) solve or a second analytic branch.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

Same excitement as before, but unproven by anything we can source. The mechanism is robust in the frictionless limit and fragile at a real leaf's friction. Pass K-L0 as frozen, with the caveat that it is passing the wrong test. The next kill must be a friction cliff: derive a Shields-type threshold for a 1–3 mm wet leaf from hand (density, size) before any visual. Settling Jar is the cheaper third sketch.

Addendum (Pawl, K-L0' map; kl0map.js, captures/keep/spoon-kl0map.txt)

Rule frozen before run: leaf moves iff sqrt2muOmr/delta > mu_fdrhogth (plate on floor, no lift, no rolling). Wall shear at Om=5: 0.32 Pa (r=R/2), 0.63 Pa (r=R). Result is a map: thickness 1 mm, drho 50-150 gives movement at mu_f<=0.2-0.3 only; at 2 mm it needs mu_f<=0.1-0.2. Leaves "never move" for mu_f>=0.5 anywhere. Reading: the sliding model is too pessimistic, since real wet leaves do move (tea-leaf paradox, observed, unsourced here); they roll, curl and lift, so a sliding plate is the wrong threshold. The map shows the pitch lives entirely on an unsourced friction/lift value, i.e. K-L0' cannot pass or fail from the desk. Not a kill, not a pass: "friction unsourced". Window if it moves: Omc 1.4-4.8 reached in 1-46 s, so the heap forms mid-spin-down, in the 10-20 s band the cuts already allow.

7-jar

Findings: sketch 7, Settling Jar

The question this sketch tested

Does silt separate from sand by at least 24 px after a 10-minute absence, with a sand band of at least 24 px at 30 s, in a jar fitted to 600 px?

What we built, and what we faked

Node only (jar.js). Stokes settling, 120 grain classes for a 40/40/20 loam, packing 0.5, no hindered settling, uniform start. Solids fraction c0 swept because nothing sources it. No capture.

What we saw (capture paths)

captures/keep/jar-kj.txt. Sand band at 30 s: 4.7 px per 1% solids (24 px needs c0 >= 5%). Silt deposited by 600 s: 2.5 px per 1%, so 12.7 px at 5%, 20.3 px at 8%. Silt grains of 20/10/2 um need 334/1334/33000 s over 12 cm. The silt layer is graded, never a separate band, and clay stays as cloud.

What we learned

The kill fires for every c0 up to 8% (about 20% by mass). It passes only near 10% solids, where hindered settling, ignored here, matters and would change the answer. By 10 min the jar reads as a sand band plus a turbid column, not two layers. The rule also makes the visitor wait on the jar's own clock.

Verdict: more or less exciting, and why

Less. Dead under the frozen bar; not tuned. Kept as a note: the honest picture is "sand falls in a minute, silt takes a lesson in patience, clay never", which is a fine sentence and a poor toy.

Critique and novelty reviews

Addendum, CRITIQUE turn 3 (after reading critique-B round 2 and the K-L2 draft)

  • I drop my "one calibrated friction value". Pawl is right: a yes/no fact admits a band, and choosing a value inside it is a lever. I accept the band rule (pass at every admitted cell). I countersigned K-L2 with amendments to the control (see notes/kl2-preregistration.md). By hand: R = 0.04 in the solvers, so Ω²r exceeds μ_f·g at Ω ≥ 10 for the admitted cells. And during spin-up, the centripetal force needs ρ_leaf, not Δρ, because the fluid is still at rest. As drafted, the control would have fired a false "solver bug".
  • "The ring is expected": conceded. The surprise is the stir, so the work must open on the heap and give the ring as the explanation. I'm no longer claiming the reverse is the original part.
  • Last sip: no rule, no sip. I've no rule I trust yet, so it stays owed and it isn't in the ranking case.
  • 1 s heap / empty skill: new kill for the grey toy, frozen now. K-V: across 3 stir styles (slow wide, fast tight, stir-and-hold), the heap's final radius or the time to form it must differ by ≥ 25%. If they don't, the only choice that matters is "stirred or not", the verb is "stir, then wait", and Spoon falls below Sounding.
  • Ranking unchanged, with conditions: 1 Spoon (K-L2 amended + K-V), 2 Sounding (revised; card capture + K-S-cold owed), Seriation → bank.

1. Sounding

Steelman. It has the best sentence in the project: your reach is the limit of what you can know. The physics is true and has one sourced constant (sun's diameter, 1/108 rad). The verb (lift a card) is one gesture that works the same on mouse and phone, and a lone visitor drives all of it. The ending is a failure that teaches: most coins stay suns. It already passed the only test that can fail, with margin, and my hand estimate (59%) matched the run, so the model is understood. The review's fixes are good and cheap. Minnaert's knotted string turns the generic chart into a record that carries information (knots high, long bare length below). A fixed date and hour give a true first-seconds cue for free: ground coins are ellipses and turn round on the card.

Kill memo. (a) It is Minnaert's experiment almost word for word (closeness 4). What we add is a framing, not a discovery. (b) The verb is a one-axis slider whose response is "everything blurs or sharpens a bit". The Under the Tree grid I opened shows how gently the image changes between h columns. Five minutes of that is a lot to ask. (c) The pass is a pass of our canopy: openness and widths are declared, not sourced. (d) It is the same tree, card and light as Under the Tree, which failed twice. Archive distance 3 (0004: a surface held to light that carries hidden information). (e) The dapple cliché is right next to it. (f) Coin overlap on A6 is unseen. Nobody has looked at a single Sounding frame.

Revision (once, as novelty-5 asks): adopt the knotted string as the record and a fixed date and hour (sun elevation → ellipse cue). Cite Minnaert and komorebi-light, and say plainly that what we add is the reach limit as the subject. Do not trace a real tree from a photo: we have no licensed photo and no helper budget. A seeded canopy stays, declared as such. Still owed before DECIDE: one grey 390×844 capture at 1.0/1.5/2.0 m. I'll append this to pitches/1.md next turn if Pawl agrees.

2. Spoon and Saucer

Steelman. It has the strongest 30-second surprise ("they went in?"), and a second verb that inverts it (turn the cup → ring). Two opposite answers from the same leaves make a rule you discover, not one you read: leaves go where the floor is slower than the water. That is a real piece of physics most people have watched and never understood. Novelty-6 found no interactive version that hands you spin-up against spin-down. Its fixes are the best in any review: a cup turned on its saucer by habit (çay glass), lifting the spoon out as the stop gesture, and the last sip as the ending. The skill pays off in a consequence (heap stays, ring washes into your mouth) with no score. The K-L0′ window (1–46 s) puts the heap inside the band the cuts allow.

Kill memo. (a) The paradox's existence in our model is decided by one unsourced number, the leaf friction/lift threshold: 0.6 s or never, a cliff. That is my 0003 failure in a new coat: a constant that decides everything, set by us. The honest defence has to be written down before any run: friction is calibrated to one observed fact (a stirred cup heaps), declared, and everything else (ring, timing window, sip) must then follow without further choice. If the ring (K-L2) or the window needs a second tuned value, it dies. (b) K-L2, the half that makes it original, is unrun. (c) Ekman Re 89–179 is above the ~55 instability, so the laminar layer is an approximation at exactly the place that matters. (d) Solver cost on a phone and a 10 s circling thumb are unmeasured. (e) Archive: the fourth "one pointer, physical surprise, then a skill" (0001, 0002, 0004). (f) The fluid-toy cliché. If the saucer doesn't invite a grip, it's a stirrer.

3. Seriation

Steelman. Archive distance 5: it is the studio's first puzzle with no clock, no physics and no held control, and the only pitch whose answer is a person, not a law. Its minute-one rule breaks for exactly one grave. The coin flipping time's direction is a real aha. K-Q5 passed (spindles are fine), and K-Q1′ passed (not trivial). Novelty-7's fix (one authored board, with the heirloom drawn worn, and the ending as the finds card turned over) removes every unsourced constant: one board, checked exhaustively by the existing branch-and-bound script.

Kill memo. (a) Dead by its own frozen rule: "a failed generator is not re-tuned; if the declared ranges fail, the pitch drops." An authored board is, by the review's own words, possibly a new pitch. (b) The finding says something true and uncomfortable: in data like this the heirloom signal (2–5 gaps) equals the noise (~2.5), so a clean answer only exists if we write it. An authored board is us hiding that. (c) No real assemblage is sourced (helpers spent), so "real Münsingen" is unavailable and the board would be invented. (d) WebGL is garnish, and the pitch admits it. (e) One board means no replay, and drag-to-sort is familiar. (f) Graves as material need careful tone.

Revision? No. It isn't a revision but a new pitch, and our rule says it drops. → idea bank as "Seriation, authored board (worn heirloom, finds card)", with the noise finding attached.

Ranking (proposed)

  1. Spoon and Saucer, conditional. Before DECIDE: (i) write the friction declaration ("one value calibrated to 'a stirred cup heaps'; no second tuned value") and freeze it; (ii) run K-L2 (spin-up → ring) as an analytic branch with that same frozen value; (iii) one grey top-down leaf capture. K-L2 fails, or needs a second tuned value → Spoon drops.
  2. Sounding, revised (string, date and hour, citations), with the grey card capture still owed.
  3. Seriation → bank.

Critique B, round 2 (Pawl, CRITIQUE turn 2, fresh eyes)

Addendum, turn 4 (no run, no image opened). Novelty-8 PASSes revised Sounding (pitch 1), so each candidate has had its one revision. K-L2: I accepted Nacre's amendments A and B but replaced 2a/2b with a density bracket (notes/kl2-preregistration.md, "Pawl's reply"). By hand, the ρ_leaf control fails at Ω = 5 at every admitted cell (≈730 vs ≤440 N/m³). So "the boundary flow alone makes the ring" is withdrawn before any run: the floor's flow and the turning push together. That weakens kill memo (c) further, because the ring is the expected half and now isn't even a clean demonstration. K-V: Nacre's styles (slow wide / fast tight) pass trivially on effort alone, which makes it a test that can't fail. My draft notes/kv-preregistration.md holds angular momentum equal and varies only the shape (wide / tight / rim). This is the real skill question. (Round-1 critique of Rubbing / Under the Tree / Capstan is in git history. Its verdict was "nothing survives". This file covers the three round-2 pitches.)

Read this turn: pitches/1–3 (I skimmed 3), novelty-5/6/7, critique-A.md, captures/keep/spoon-kl0map.txt. I opened no image this turn. I accept Nacre's report that no frame of Sounding, Spoon or Seriation exists, but I have not checked it with my own eyes. Every number below is node output already in the repo or my own hand arithmetic, marked [hand].

1. Sounding

Steelman. One sourced constant (the sun at 1/108 rad) and one gesture that a lone visitor fully owns. It has the only frozen test of the three that could fail and didn't (K-S2 58.7% vs ⅓), and Nacre's hand estimate predicted it, so we understand the model. The ending is a failure that teaches. The knotted string turns the record into a consequence: the bare length is literally what you couldn't reach. That is the kind of record I trust. A fixed date and hour give a free, true first cue: ground ellipses turn round on the level card.

Kill memo. (a) The stake "your reach is the limit" exists only in the story. On screen the visitor's arm limits nothing. We put a ceiling at 2.0 m, and the drag simply stops there. A sourced median reach is still a lever we set, and nothing the visitor does with skill can push it. That is precisely what my taste says to distrust: stakes that exist only in the story. (b) The verb is a one-axis slider, and the response is mostly a uniform shrink. A resolve is a per-gap threshold, so a "sounding" is one coin slowly going odd over a ~9 mm/m line. Under the Tree's h-grid (Nacre opened it) shows how gentle that change is. (c) It is Minnaert's experiment (closeness 4) plus a framing. What we add is a sentence. (d) The canopy, openness and widths are ours, and halving density puts K-S2 near the line (~36%). (e) The family has already failed twice (K-T1, K-T2), and archive distance is 3 (0004).

Revision (once): I accept Nacre's version: the string, the date and hour, citations, and no photo tree. I add one requirement: the pitch must say plainly that the ceiling is chosen, and the about text must not claim the visitor's body is the limit. If the string's bare length can't carry the feeling once the ceiling is admitted to be ours, Sounding has no subject left.

2. Spoon and Saucer

Steelman. It has the only true reversal on the table. The same leaves give opposite answers to two gestures, and the rule ("leaves go where the floor is slower than the water") is discovered, not read. Novelty-6 found no browser version where you play spin-up against spin-down. Its fixes are concrete: lift the spoon out to stop, use a cup turned on a saucer by habit, and end with the last sip. The skill then pays off in a consequence, with no score. That is the best ending any pitch this project has had.

Kill memo. (a) Friction decides whether the paradox exists in our model (kl0map: 1 s, 46 s, or NEVER across μ_f × size × Δρ). That is 0003's failure again. (b) The pitch's clock is wrong (novelty-6). The heap forms in about 1 s or not at all, not over a 27 s creep, and the 3–5 min timing skill was written on the wrong clock. If the heap is near-instant, the skill "stop at the right moment" may be empty. (c) The ring may be the unsurprising half. "Turn the cup and the leaves go outward" is what everyone expects. So the reversal's surprise rests entirely on the stir. The ring is still worth having as a contrast, but the novelty claim ("the reverse is the original part") overstates it. (d) The last-sip physics is a story so far: no rule says ring leaves wash out and heap leaves stay. It needs a written rule before it is promised. (e) The Ekman layer is laminar at Re 89–179, which is above the ~55 instability. Phone solver cost and the 10 s thumb circling are unmeasured. (f) It is the fourth "one pointer, one physical surprise, then a skill" (0001, 0002, 0004), and it sits next to the fluid-toy cliché.

On Nacre's question 2: is "one friction value calibrated to an observed fact" honest? Not as phrased. "A stirred cup heaps" is a binary fact, so it doesn't pin a value. It admits a whole band of the map. If we pick one value from that band, we have chosen a lever and only dressed it as calibration. The honest version uses the band: K-L2 must pass at every cell of the existing kl0map grid that satisfies the heap fact, not at one value we pick. Then there is nothing to tune, because the grid already exists and was not made for K-L2. Draft: notes/kl2-preregistration.md. I'll co-sign it once Nacre agrees with the wording or counters it.

A useful check [hand]: does a dense leaf on a turning floor slide outward by centrifugal force alone, which would make the ring trivial? It slides when Ω²r > μ_f·g. At Ω = 5, r = 3 cm, Ω²r = 0.75 m/s², while μ_f·g = 0.98–7.8 m/s² for μ_f 0.1–0.8. So no: with any friction in the grid, the floor carries the leaf around and only the outward boundary flow can move it. The free-slip control in K-L2 is therefore a real control, not a formality.

3. Seriation

Steelman. Archive distance 5. It is our first clockless puzzle, and its answer is a person, not a law. The coin that flips time's direction is a real "aha".

Kill memo. Dead by its own frozen rule (K-Q2 42.5% vs 50%, K-Q3 7.5% vs 80%). An authored board is a new pitch. Novelty-7 says so too: the authored board is what the generator failed to produce, so writing it by hand repairs the outcome, not the mechanism. The finding that matters is that the heirloom signal (2–5 gaps) is about as large as sampling noise (~2.5). A clean authored answer hides that finding, and that would be deception by design. It also has no sourced assemblage, and its WebGL is garnish.

Verdict: I agree with Nacre. Bank it, with the noise finding attached. One idea for the bank, honestly a different pitch: "the heirloom you can't prove". A piece whose subject is that the signal is the size of the noise, where the visitor's best ordering is defensible but never certain.

4. Ranking

  1. Spoon and Saucer, conditional on notes/kl2-preregistration.md being co-signed and frozen before any run. Then, in order: the K-L2 band run, a written last-sip rule (or drop the sip and say so), and one grey top-down capture. If K-L2 fails at any heap-admitting cell, or needs a value outside the existing grid, Spoon drops. No second attempt.
  2. Sounding, revised (string, date and hour, citations, and the chosen ceiling admitted), still owing its grey 390×844 card capture at 1.0/1.5/2.0 m and a cold read of that capture.
  3. Seriation → bank.

If both conditions fail, nothing survives, and we say so as we did in round 1. Two rounds with nothing surviving would itself be a finding about how we ideate: we keep pitching true phenomena with a gentle response curve.

Novelty review: Pitch 1, Rubbing (novelty-1)

Note: the findings I was handed are for sketch 1, Under the Tree, a different pitch. I judged Rubbing against sketches/2-rubbing/FINDINGS.md. Strict K-R2 failed at f ≤ 0.3. There is a real trade-off with a 3–40× pressure gap for f ≥ 0.5. Lichen and the cold read were not run.

Nearest existing works
  • **Felix Gonzalez-Torres, Untitled (Portrait of Ross in L.A.)**: visitors take candy and the work shrinks. Taking costs the work. Closeness 2.
  • Rule-based participatory art (Aeon essay: cut-the-string piece, chewed chocolate): one visitor's act permanently changes the work for the next. Closeness 2.
  • Chain World (Rohrer): one copy of a world, with changes that persist for later players. Closeness 1.
  • Heaven's Vault: reading worn inscriptions and completing them from context. Closeness 1.
  • Graves and grass (itch.io): tending graves in the browser, with no rubbing or cost. Closeness 1.

Searches found no interactive work about rubbing at all, or about reading that wears away what it reads. The concept looks genuinely open.

Archive

The nearest past work is 0004 Breath Figure. It shares wiping a surface to see, traces left by strangers, and a single light as the only UI. The pitch's "reverse" (permanent, not healing) is real but thin. 0001 Phantom Jam shares the moral "the gentle hand wins". That would make it the studio's third work whose lesson is restraint.

Scores

| Concept | Experience | Coherence | Cliché distance | Archive distance | Ambition/feasibility | |---|---|---|---|---|---| | 4 | 3 | 4 | 4 | 3 | 3 |

The average is 3.5 and no score is at or below 2, so this is a PASS, but only just. Experience is held at 3 because a mouse has no pressure, and pressure is the whole choice.

Most generic aspects, made specific
  1. "The cursor is the wax": the pitch never says what pressure is on a mouse. Make it specific: pressure = how many passes you make over the same spot in a short window. Hard rubbing is then literally scrubbing back and forth, and that is the greedy gesture itself. Use PointerEvent.pressure only on pen.
  2. "Everyone before you rubbed it": as written, the wear is a made-up backstory. Make it specific: make the stone persistent and shared, so you rub what real past visitors left. If that's not possible, show their sheets pinned in a porch, dated, palest to darkest.
  3. Atmosphere of a generic lichened churchyard: Make it specific: use a real, widely repeated epitaph as the famous line (e.g. the 18th–19th c. English verse "Afflictions sore long time I bore…"). Then the "repeats on neighbouring stones" mechanic is a historical fact, not a device. Make the obscure line one ordinary name and trade.
Must address
  • Scope the pitch to f ≥ 0.5 openly, as a new question rather than a rescue, or drop the claim that the effect "falls out of geometry" for all lines.
  • Separate the work from 0004's single-light framing and from the restraint moral in 0001/0004. For example, let the greedy dark sheet be the more beautiful object, so the choice really costs something.

novelty-2: REVISE

Novelty review, pitch 2: Under the Tree (novelty-2)

Note: the findings I was given (sketches/2-rubbing/FINDINGS.md) are for a different pitch, Rubbing. The sketch that tests this pitch is sketches/1-under-the-tree/FINDINGS.md, so I read that one too. Its K-T1 kill test failed, and so did its K-T2 re-test with the verb reframed. Under the Tree is now in reserve.

Nearest existing works
  • BAAS 2024, "Lessons from Dappled Light During a Solar Eclipse": a paper with a free app that simulates leaf pinhole images, including how they behave during an eclipse. It treats the effect as a convolution and finds that small gaps resolve crescents while large gaps stay blobs. That is this pitch's mechanism, though it is a research tool rather than an experience. Closeness 3. https://baas.aas.org/pub/2024n9i017/release/1
  • MIT thesis, "Komorebi: Embedding Dappled Sunlight in the Built Environment": design research on how screen distance and gap size shape the spots. Closeness 2. https://dspace.mit.edu/bitstream/handle/1721.1/129914/1237109786-MIT.pdf?isAllowed=y&sequence=1
  • "Komorebi", Lung Fu Shan exhibition (HKU): an artist records round sun spots under trees on paper, site-specific and tied to the time of day. It is the card as the instrument, done as a physical installation. Closeness 2. https://www.lungfushanexhibition.hku.hk/komorebi
  • Poke-A-Pixel: a Blender render of eclipse crescents under a tree. It shows the image, with no interaction. Closeness 2. https://poke-a-pixel.tumblr.com/post/150890002144/using-blender-to-simulate-the-pinhole-camera
  • Ciechanowski, "Cameras and Lenses": pinhole optics explained through diagrams and sliders. Closeness 1. https://ciechanow.ski/cameras-and-lenses/

None of these lets you hold the screen and find the distance where the spots turn into the sun. The idea is unclaimed.

Archive

The nearest past work is 0004 Breath Figure: a surface you work on where light reveals hidden information. Structurally the pitch also repeats 0001 and 0002: one simple input, plus an event on its own clock that disturbs what you're doing. That makes three works out of four built on the formula "one control plus a perturbation you can't control".

Scores

| Criterion | Score | |---|---| | Concept | 4 | | Experience | 3 | | Coherence | 4 | | Distance from clichés | 4 | | Distance from archive | 3 | | Ambition vs. feasibility | 2 |

The concept is sharp: noticing that the round coins are all the same size is a real discovery for a visitor. But the pitch sells its experience on the chase, and the studio's own simulation says that chase doesn't happen:

  • The band doesn't move enough. It shifts only 1.2–1.9× at 2% openness and never opens at 5% or more.
  • Few crescents at deep eclipse. Only 4% of spots read as crescents at m=0.8.
  • The big gaps never resolve. They hold most of the light and stay blobs at every height.

What's left is a focus knob plus a show, which is exactly the risk the pitch names itself.

The three most generic aspects
  1. "A tree", "an eclipse", "its own clock". Use one real event: the 12 Aug 2026 eclipse at a named place under a named species (the ESA photo exists). Take the magnitude curve and the direction of the bite from the real ephemeris, with real minutes scaled to real seconds.
  2. Card height used as a focus slider. The sim says the effect belongs to individual gaps, not to a height band. Give the hand a second, physical axis. Tilting the card turns coins into ellipses. Or let the visitor pick one gap and follow its spot, making the unit "this hole" instead of "the whole card".
  3. Warm midsummer dappled-light mood, the komorebi cliché. Build the mood from what only an eclipse does. Near maximum, leaf shadows go sharp along one axis, the light turns cold and flat while the crescents stay bright, and a still, sharp-edged shadow is the hush. That dimming is the material, and it needs an exposure rule taken from measured sky brightness, not an invented one.
Verdict

REVISE. The idea is original and specific, and nobody has made it. But the interaction it promises has been falsified in the studio's own sketch. Revise it around the per-gap reading the sketch actually found, or around a different verb such as tilt, and re-sketch before treating it as a lead. Otherwise it stays in reserve.

Novelty review: Capstan (pitch 3)

Nearest existing works

  • PhySandbox Capstan simulator: μ/φ sliders and a T₂/T₁ plot. It has the law but not the hand. 2/5
  • FIRGELLI capstan calculator: an animated rope on a drum with turns and tail tension. There's no motion and no load. 2/5
  • The Boat Docker: a browser docking sim where you click to attach lines to cleats. Lines are binary and there's no friction. 2/5
  • Harvard rope-around-pole demo: 4½ turns hold a wrecking ball. It's the same "weak hand, huge load" wonder, but physical and static. 2/5
  • Sailwind: you moor by clicking a line onto a bollard. Same scene, no mechanic. 1/5

I found no work where the wrap angle itself is the input and checking a moving load by surging is the skill. In concept it is genuinely open ground.

Archive overlap. The nearest is 0001 Phantom Jam, not 0002. Both are one-input braking where the virtue is a gentle, graded hold rather than a hard stop ("only a gentle hold dissolves it" vs "the skill is the surge"). 0002 Plain Hunt shares the held rope. This is the studio's third "modulate one held control gently" piece, so archive distance is only middling.

Scores: concept 4, experience 4, coherence 4, cliché distance 5, archive distance 3, ambition/feasibility 3 (avg 3.83).

The idea is sharp. A fixed-strength fingertip turns e^(μθ) into a bodily fact, and circling the bollard really is the wrap. The scorch record is a good final image that grows out of the mechanic. Feasibility is the weak point: K-C1 strictly failed (75.5% docks, 7.4% parts against ≥90% and <5%), the masses and break load are assumed rather than sourced, and the phone jog-wheel was never tested.

Three most generic aspects

  1. The "gentle hand beats hard hand" moral repeats Phantom Jam. Make the hard case the point instead: give one barge too much momentum for any surge to save. The only good outcome is choosing what to sacrifice (the line or the quay), and the scorch record shows that choice.
  2. A grey overcast harbour is a stock "tense and melancholy" mood. Pin it to one real quay, tide state and rope type (a sourced Thames lighter on hemp, or a canal narrowboat on a cast-iron dolly). Let that real μ and the wet or dry rope be what the visitor gets to read.
  3. Barges arriving at varied speeds is a level sequence and drifts toward the "mini-game with a score" risk. Make it one continuous evening: the same rope, with scorched sections weakening it so that later dockings must avoid earlier burns. The record then becomes a constraint, not just a readout.

novelty-4: re-review of pitch 2, Under the Tree (revised)

Note: the findings I was given (2-rubbing) belong to a different pitch. The sketch for this pitch is sketches/1-under-the-tree/FINDINGS.md: its K-T1 test failed, and so did the K-T2 re-test. The revision itself has no sketch.

Nearest works
  • BAAS 2024, "Lessons from Dappled Light During a Solar Eclipse" (3/5): a paper with a free app that simulates leaf pinhole images as a convolution. It finds that small gaps resolve crescents and large gaps stay blobs. https://baas.aas.org/pub/2024n9i017/release/1
  • HartRAO eclipse pinhole test (3/5): triangular holes of 2–10 mm projected at 30 cm, 1 m and 2 m. Smaller holes turn round sooner. That is exactly the revised "follow one gap" rule, done as a physical demo. http://www.hartrao.ac.za/other/eclipse2002/pinhole.html
  • NASA 2024 pinhole projector activity (2/5): learners vary hole size and distance. https://science.nasa.gov/wp-content/uploads/2024/01/2024-total-solar-eclipse-pinhole-projector-activity.pdf
  • Komorebi, Lung Fu Shan exhibition (HKU) (2/5): sun spots under trees recorded on paper by hand. https://www.lungfushanexhibition.hku.hk/komorebi
  • Ciechanowski, "Cameras and Lenses" (1/5): pinhole optics explained with sliders. https://ciechanow.ski/cameras-and-lenses/
Archive

The nearest past work is 0004 Breath Figure: light on a surface that carries information. The pitch also keeps the formula of 0001 and 0002: one control plus an event on its own clock. That makes four works out of five with this shape.

Assessment

The revision drops the falsified chase and adopts the per-gap reading from the sketch, which is honest. But it moves the experience closer to the classroom lab:

  • The rule is a textbook rule. "Tap a gap, raise the card, a smaller gap comes round sooner" is what HartRAO and NASA already teach with cardboard.
  • The eclipse half is weak. K-T2 found only 4% of gaps reading as crescents at m=0.8.
  • The mood rule is unsourced. The exposure rule it depends on has no measured source.
  • No sketch or test. The authors themselves recommend reserve.

The 30-second noticing ("the round ones are all the same size") is still the strongest original moment, and nobody has built it as a held-card web piece.

The three most generic aspects
  1. Tap one gap and follow its spot. This is a demo of a known rule. Make it specific by inverting the task: you see the card's coins, and you must find which leaf gap made which coin. The leaves become the mystery and the card becomes the evidence. Gaps that never resolve are the answer, not a failure.
  2. One control plus a clock. Give the visitor a second physical axis instead. Tilting the card stretches the coins into ellipses whose long axis points to the sun's elevation. Or drop the timed eclipse and let the visitor scrub the real 12 Aug 2026 minute.
  3. A cold, dimming mood rule that is still unsourced. Either source it (measured illuminance curves from eclipses do exist) or cut it. The mood can come from the still, sharpening leaf shadows near maximum instead.
Verdict

REVISE. The idea is unclaimed but the second minute is thin, the revised form has no sketch, and it repeats the archive's dominant structure. Re-sketch the inverted or tilt version, or leave it in reserve.

novelty-5: Sounding (pitch 1, revised): REVISE

Nearest existing works

  • **Minnaert, Light and Colour in the Open Air, §1 "Sun-Pictures": "Intercept one of these images by a piece of paper… Raise the paper higher and the spot grows smaller." He also measures the distance to the opening with string. The pitch's verb is this book's experiment, almost word for word. Closeness 4**.
  • komorebi-light (sandypjoshi, three.js): leaf light on paper in the browser. The shader integrates over the sun's disc through canopies at several depths, so far gaps cast round suns. It has the same physics and material, but no verb. Closeness 3.
  • HartRAO pinhole page / Exploratorium "Spot the Sun": a card held at 30 cm, 1 m and 2 m shows the hole's shape go to a round sun. The size threshold is the lesson. Closeness 3.
  • BAAS 2024, "Lessons from Dappled Light During a Solar Eclipse": a dappled-light simulation built from real foliage images. Closeness 2.
  • Poke-A-Pixel Blender render of leaf pinholes: a render, not interactive. Closeness 1.

What is actually new: the framing. Your reach is the limit of what you can know, and most coins stay round. The verb and the physics are textbook (Minnaert). The pitch's "no interactive piece" claim holds, but only narrowly, and it should cite Minnaert and komorebi-light.

Archive: the nearest work is 0004 Breath Figure. It also uses a surface you hold up to light, where the light carries hidden information, and a single gesture as the UI. The daylight and the soundings chart make it different, but not far.

Evidence: the attached contact sheet is from Under the Tree (K-T1/K-T2 failed), not Sounding. K-S1″ is admitted not to be a test. K-S2, the only kill that can fail, is unrun. Coin overlap on A6 is unsourced.

Scores: concept 3, experience 3, coherence 4, cliché distance 4, archive distance 3, ambition/feasibility 3. The average is 3.33, below the 3.5 needed to pass, so this is REVISE.

Most generic aspects, made specific

  1. A seeded hidden canopy with log-uniform gaps. Make it specific: trace one real tree, with a named species, crown height and date, from a photo looking up through it, as the BAAS work does. "The shape of the gap above you" then becomes a real gap.
  2. A "chart of soundings" strip at the card's edge. This is generic UI. Make it specific: use Minnaert's knotted string. Each resolve ties a knot at that height, and you leave with the string: knots near the top, and a long bare length for everything you couldn't reach.
  3. A generic "summer noon" mood. Make it specific: fix a real place, date and hour. The sun's elevation then makes the ground coins ellipses that turn round on the level card, which Minnaert notes and which is a free, true first-seconds cue. The sun's real angular size for that date (±1.7%) then sets the 1/108.

Must do: run K-S2 as frozen and attach a Sounding capture. Then add Minnaert and komorebi-light to the nearest-works section and say plainly what the work adds to them.

novelty-6: re-review of pitch 2, Spoon and Saucer: PASS (borderline, average 3.5)

Note: the findings I was given (2-rubbing) belong to a different pitch, Rubbing. I used sketches/6-spoon/FINDINGS.md instead.

Nearest works
  • MIT Weather in a Tank, Ekman layers (4/5): a turntable demo where spin-down sends bottom dye inward and spin-up sends it outward. That is the pitch's two-verb inversion, done as a lab demo with dials, not hands. https://weathertank.mit.edu/links/projects/ekman-layers-introduction/ekman-layers-tank-how-to
  • "Tea Leaf Paradox Revisited: Aggregation in Rings" (3/5): COMSOL simulations plus videos showing dot, ring and edge aggregation, which depend on the vessel and the rotation. https://www.ncbi.nlm.nih.gov/pmc/articles/PMC10672922/
  • Culinary fluid mechanics review, Heavers & Dapp cake pan (3/5): spin-up dye spirals outward on the bottom. https://arxiv.org/pdf/2201.12128
  • Tea leaf paradox on Wikipedia, plus stir videos (3/5): the stir half only. https://en.wikipedia.org/wiki/Tea_leaf_paradox
  • "Science of a coffee cup" (2/5): a movie of leaves gathering, with boundary-layer analysis. https://arxiv.org/pdf/2501.01180

I found no browser version where you play spin-up against spin-down by hand. The pitch's claim that nobody hands you the reverse is true for interactive works, but not for lab demos (MIT shows it).

Archive

The nearest past work is 0004 Breath Figure: a domestic surface simulated under one light, where a hand gesture reveals a physics. It also overlaps 0001 Phantom Jam: a ring geometry, and a skill built on timing a gentle stop. This would be the fourth work out of four built on "one pointer, one physical surprise, then a skill".

Assessment

"They went in?" is a real moment that nobody has built for the browser. The saucer as a second verb is the sharpest idea in the pitch. Weaknesses:

  • The pitch's clock is wrong. The sketch finds the heap forms in about 1 s, or never, depending on leaf friction. It does not "creep" in over the 27 s e-fold. The 30-second description and the 3–5 minute timing skill both rest on that clock.
  • Friction is unsourced. The friction/lift threshold decides whether the paradox exists at all in the model.
  • Two tests are unrun. K-L1′ (the free-slip control) and K-L2 (turning the cup) have not been run, and K-L2 is the half that makes this original.
The three most generic aspects
  1. "White cup, amber tea, morning light." This is stock-photo domesticity. Choose a cup that is turned on its saucer by habit: a tulip glass of Turkish çay on its saucer, or a gaiwan, with the rim worn where fingers turn it. The saucer verb then comes from the object rather than from the physics lesson.
  2. The stir as a circling drag body force. This is the "paint with fluid" verb. Make the spoon a visible object you dip in, and make stopping mean lifting the spoon out, with the clink. The stop is the key event, so give it its own gesture.
  3. "Gather every leaf into one ring, then one heap." This goal is bolted on like a puzzle. Give it a tea-drinker's purpose instead: the coda is the last sip. You tilt the cup to drink. Leaves in the heap stay put, and leaves left in the ring wash into your mouth. That rewards the skill without a score and ends the piece.
Verdict

PASS, with conditions:

  • Run K-L2 before anything visual.
  • Fix the clock in the pitch.
  • Source a rolling or lift threshold for wet leaves, or drop the friction term openly.
