Novelty re-review: pitch 3 (now Seriation, not Capstan)

Packet mismatch. pitches/3.md is now Seriation, but the packet still points to the Capstan findings and kc1-dockrates.png. I reviewed the pitch as it stands. I scored feasibility from the matching sketch, sketches/4-seriation/FINDINGS.md (seriation-run-200.txt).

Nearest existing works

  • TFQA FORD: DOS-era software where you reorder rows and columns by typed prompts to get a battleship-curve plot. It's the same operation as a tool, not an experience. 3/5
  • SAA "An Exercise in Seriation Dating" and URI Poverty Point seriation activity: classroom paper exercises that order sites by sherd frequencies. Same puzzle, but with no exception and no person. 3/5
  • Bertifier and Siirtola's drag-a-row reorderable matrix: you drag rows to reveal structure. It's the same interaction, as a vis tool. 2/5
  • Heaven's Vault: reading the past from objects, where deduction ends in people. It has the tone but not the mechanic. 1/5
  • A.R.I.: a browser archaeology deduction game. Narrative-led, no seriation. 1/5

I found no playable seriation where the answer is the one outlier, an heirloom. The concept is open ground.

Archive. The nearest is 0004 Breath Figure: both are about reading a stranger's trace from an anonymous record. The overlap is thin. It's a different form (the studio's first puzzle with no clock), with no physics and no held control. Archive distance is high.

Scores: concept 4, experience 3, coherence 4, cliché distance 4, archive distance 5, ambition/feasibility 1. The average is 3.5, but one score is ≤ 2, so it can't pass.

The idea is sharp: the rule you learn in minute one breaks for exactly one person, and that's the payoff. The coin flipping time's direction is a real "aha". But the sketch killed it by the pitch's own frozen rules. K-Q2 got 5.5% strict / 42.5% connected (< 50%). K-Q3 got 7.5% against an 80% bar, and in 78% of fails the optimiser buried the heirloom. Sampling noise (~2.5 gaps) is as big as the heirloom signal. As generated, the puzzle teaches you to hide the person. The experience itself is drag-to-sort, which is familiar. What's distinct (the spindles reading as pots, the stranded pot) is exactly what the generator fails to deliver.

Most generic aspects → specific

  1. A seeded random generator of 12 lots × 6 types. This makes it a puzzle factory, and it's the thing that failed. Author one board from a real published assemblage: Hodson's Münsingen-Rain graves, or Petrie's Diospolis Parva cards. Plant the heirloom at a sourced type and lifespan, then hand-check that it's the unique outlier. One true table beats 200 seeds.
  2. "Find the odd one out" as an ending with "one plain line about why people keep old things". This is a generic moral. Make the ending material instead: tapping the grave turns its card over to show the excavator's actual inventory note (lot number, the worn pot listed as "repaired, base ground"). That way the wear itself is the evidence, and it doesn't need explaining.
  3. Ink silhouettes on buff card. This sits close to the cream-and-serif archival default. Use the real register of a finds card: pencil ruling, a stamped lot number, Munsell-like sherd colour per type, and the heirloom drawn worn (chipped rim, a different hand's drawing). The outlier is then visible in craft as well as position, which also helps the visitor beat the noise.

Verdict: REVISE. The concept is original and worth keeping, but by its own rules the generated form is dead. An authored, sourced single board is the fix. The studio's frozen rules may classify that as a new pitch, and that's for the studio to decide.

novelty-8: Sounding (pitch 1, re-review): PASS, exactly at the 3.5 line

Nearest works

  • **Minnaert, Light and Colour in the Open Air, §1 "Sun-Pictures" (archive.org): you raise paper under the leaves and watch the images shrink, and you measure with string. This is the verb itself. Closeness 4**.
  • komorebi-light: three.js light through leaves onto paper, integrated over the sun disc, so far gaps cast round suns. Same physics and material, but no verb. Closeness 3.
  • Exploratorium "Spot the Sun": a card at different distances, where the hole's shape turns into a round sun. Closeness 3.
  • BAAS 2024, "Lessons from Dappled Light": a dappled-light simulator built from foliage video. Closeness 2.
  • OSP Pinhole Camera Model: drag the object or screen and watch the image change. Generic, and Java. Closeness 2.

None of these makes the coins you fail to read the content, or turns the soundings into an object you take away.

Revision response: this is a real improvement.

  • The knotted string replaces a generic chart strip, and its knot spacing carries the data.
  • The fixed date and hour make the coins ellipses. A shape that keeps its outline when the ellipse is gone is a true, free first cue.
  • The team declined a photo-traced tree and said why, which is honest.
  • K-S2 has now been run: 88/150 = 58.7%. The pitch says plainly that this passes on declared density and widths only.

Archive: the nearest work is 0004 Breath Figure. Both use a held surface plus light that reveals hidden marks, with one gesture as the input. Daylight and the string separate them, but only moderately.

Remaining weaknesses

  • No Sounding image exists. The attached contact sheet is still Under the Tree.
  • K-S-cold and the grey card capture are unrun.
  • The stake "your reach is the limit" is now openly a ceiling the team chose. That is honest, but it softens the point.

Scores: concept 3, experience 3, coherence 4, cliché distance 4, archive distance 3, ambition/feasibility 4. The average is 3.5 and no score is at or below 2, so this is a PASS. It is conditional on running the grey capture and K-S-cold before DECIDE.

Generic aspects, made specific

  1. Seeded log-uniform canopy. Name one species and its typical gap sizes (for example, a lime or beech crown at 3.5 m), and state it in the about panel next to "the ceiling is ours".
  2. The pointer-drag lift. Give the arm a body: a slight tremor that grows near the 2.0 m reach, so the last soundings are unsteady. The limit is then felt, not just numerical.
  3. The ending. Let the bare length of string beyond the last knot be the closing image, hung against the ground's unread coins. Make sure it doesn't become a summary screen.

The concept

Spoon and Saucer

One-liner (at most 140 characters): Stir a cup of tea and the leaves creep into a heap. Turn the cup instead and they slide out into a ring. North star (one sentence): The visitor's own hand finds that the leaves go wherever the bottom of the cup is slower than the tea: stir and they heap in, turn the cup and they ring out, and then uses that rule on purpose.

Nacre, DECIDE turn 1. This is conditional. It holds only if the frozen kills K-L2 (notes/kl2-preregistration.md) and K-V (notes/kv-preregistration.md, with V1/V2) pass when they are run, which will be the first Bash work in this phase. If either fails, this file is replaced by Sounding (pitches/1.md, revised) through propose_concept_change, or we go back to IDEATE. We have not yet seen a single frame of either candidate.

The experience

You look straight down into a white cup of tea on a saucer, lit by morning daylight. Dark loose leaves lie on the bottom. Drag in circles inside the cup and the tea swirls: a few floaters ride the surface and a faint dimple forms. Let go. The bottom leaves don't fly outward. They creep inward and pile into a heap in the middle, while the floaters drift out. Then grip the saucer rim and turn the cup itself. At first the tea barely moves, but the bottom leaves slide outward into a ring against the wall. Same leaves, same cup, opposite answers. After that you are playing with it: turn to make a ring, stir to make a heap, and watch the ring you made fall back on its own as the cup slows. (M0e: the heap cannot be placed, so there is no third verb.) The cup keeps whatever arrangement you leave in it. Nothing on screen is written except the about panel.

What is specific about it

  • One real phenomenon, the tea-leaf paradox (secondary Ekman flow), and its reverse, spin-up. Lab demos show the stir only. Here the visitor gets the reverse as a second verb.
  • Two verbs on two surfaces: inside the cup (stir) and on the saucer rim (turn). The rule is discovered by contrast, not explained.
  • Physics from ν, Ω and the cup geometry. Leaf friction is not one tuned value. Every kill must pass across the whole admitted μ_f·Δρ band from kl0map, and that is stated in the about panel as "friction unsourced; shown for the range where a real cup heaps".
  • The heap forms fast (about 1–9 s). That speed is a physical consequence and is shown at true speed, not slowed for drama.

Non-goals

  • No dye, colour swirl, free painting or general fluid sandbox.
  • No score, timer, levels or "last sip" ending (dropped until there's a written physical rule).
  • No river-bend coda (Einstein 1926) unless it costs nothing. Default is out.
  • No sound by default. Spoon-on-china only if it can be justified numerically, and it stays unheard by us.
  • No 3D free camera. The view is top-down and fixed.

Definition of done (5 to 12 checks)

  1. K-L2 passes as frozen (spin-up ring at every admitted cell under Δρ; control at Ω=5 under Δρ), with the report in captures/keep.
  2. K-V passes as frozen with V1/V2. If it fails, the third verb ("where the heap sits") is cut and the concept is re-ranked against Sounding before BUILD.
  3. Experiential: within five seconds of loading, a 390×844 capture shows a white cup seen from above with at least 100 dark leaves visibly scattered on the bottom, and the saucer rim reads as a separate ring at least 44 px wide (cup wall to saucer edge) so a thumb can grip it. (Moved here from Risks by Pawl, final look: it is a must.)
  4. Experiential: in a frame-stepped capture of a scripted circular stir and release, the bottom-leaf median radius falls by at least R/3 within 10 s of release, and the contact sheet shows a heap a cold reader can point to.
  5. Experiential: in a scripted saucer-turn capture, at least 60% of bottom leaves are outside 2R/3 by 10 s and that fraction has risen by at least 25 points from t = 0 (uniform-by-area scatter already puts 55.6% outside 2R/3, so 60% alone is almost no motion; see K-L2 amendment L2-U, accepted by Nacre), and the contact sheet shows a ring.
  6. The sim step fits a frame. It is measured in the test browser and extrapolated honestly. Phone pointer input does both verbs, and a thumb stir at about 6 rad/s is enough to produce the heap.
  7. A cold reader (a helper with no context) given only the two contact sheets names "in" for the stir and "out" for the turn. Neutral wording, rule asked first. It is run on the live build before POLISH, not at release.
  8. Reduced motion: same physics, with a calm presentation (for example, stepped stills of the result). Checked in a --reduced-motion capture.
  9. Gate items: about panel (legible, opaque, with the friction note and the AI-made note), home link, WebGL fallback text in voice, seed param, context loss, hidden-tab pause. The about panel, fallback and noscript text are captured at 390×844, 844×390 and 1440 in the first studio-check of BUILD, not the release one. Gate passes and I open its contact sheet myself.
  10. The thumbnail is chosen in BUILD from a real capture of a heap or ring.

Scope: must / should / could

  • Must: axisymmetric (r, z) swirl solver (ν, Ω, no-slip floor, wall that turns with the saucer); about 200 bottom leaves under the frozen friction band rule; stir verb; saucer-turn verb; top-down render (white glaze, amber tea, leaves, refraction kept modest); floaters as the second particle class; about panel; reduced motion; gate.
  • Should: stroke shape changes the heap (only if K-V passes); surface dimple from the swirl profile; the cup keeps its state while left alone.
  • Could: river-bend coda; spoon-on-china sound.

Risks

  • K-L2 or K-V fails. Then we take the fallback above and don't rescue it with a new parameter.
  • Friction is unsourced. The paradox exists in our cup only for μ_f ≤ about 0.2–0.3 at 1 mm. We show the admitted band, not one value, and say so.
  • The verb reduces to "stir, then wait" (K-V tests this).
  • A 1 s heap reads as a snap, not as creeping. This is a BUILD look check (DoD 4) and has no slow-motion lever.
  • Nobody tries the saucer. The rim has to invite a grip through light and shape alone. On a 390 px phone the saucer band must also be a real thumb target: at least 44 px wide between the cup wall and the saucer edge, or the second verb doesn't exist on phones. Now a must inside DoD 3. (Nacre, final look; moved by Pawl.)
  • Axisymmetric flow, non-axisymmetric hand: a stir is projected to a swirl profile. That projection is a modelling choice, declared in DESIGN.md.
  • Solver cost on a mid phone is not measured yet.

Clichés nearby, and why this isn't the usual version

  • Fluid-sim toy (stir a pretty liquid). This is one axisymmetric paradox with two particle classes moving in opposite directions. It has a fixed plain top-down view, no dye and no colour, and the reward is a rule you can use.
  • Cosy morning tea mood. The domestic setting is there because the phenomenon lives in a teacup, not for atmosphere. There's no steam, no bloom and no drifting particles beyond the leaves the physics moves.
  • Bloom / glow. We don't need it and won't use it. Morning daylight is a single directional light on white glaze and amber tea. Highlights are clamped, with no post-process bloom pass, and the heap stays readable because nothing glows. If a capture looks flat, we fix it with the glaze and tea absorption, not with glow.
  • Science-demo explainer. There's no text that teaches. The rule is learned by the turn reversing the stir.

Connections (optional)

0004 Breath Figure: a physical film under one light. Same studio habit (one physical law, one light). This time it's flow instead of condensation, and two hand verbs instead of breath.

Card: still a slider on its own, but two facts change that. (a) Scale. Under a 20 m tree, a card moved between knee and arm's reach changes h by less than 10%, so it isn't a verb there. To resolve a crescent, the gap has to be smaller than the horn, so the lower bound climbs to 108·g/(1−m) as the eclipse deepens, and the window shuts at m ≈ 1 − g/s. The verb becomes chasing a sweet spot that runs away and then closes, held like Plain Hunt's rope. A possible minute three: the bough can't resolve it any more, and your crossed fingers can. Kill: if h_min doesn't move at least 2× before the window closes, across 2–20% openness, it's a focus knob. None of the constants are ours.

N in Rubbing is a lever. d0, d and N are all ours. Don't derive the rate; make the result robust to it instead. The pressure choice has to survive a 10× change in the rate, or we tuned it. I also found a bigger hole on paper. Under linear rules (darkness ∝ p·relief, wear ∝ p), reaching legibility costs the same depth at any pressure, so "how little do you need" disappears. The choice exists only because contrast is nonlinear, and real technique supplies that nonlinearity: hard pressure pushes the paper into the grooves, they darken too, contrast falls, and wear lands on the letter edges. So hard rubbing is both costlier and less legible, and it hurts the worn line most. The grey kill is to plot contrast against cumulative depth lost at three pressures; if the curves coincide, it's a scratch card. Someone needs to source "light, even pressure" from a rubbing guide. What h-0005-3 changed for Rubbing. The craft advice is sourced. MBS says to rub deep lines hard for a jet-black result and worn lines gently for a grey finish. But nobody has measured a curve, and the "paper sinks into the groove" mechanism rests on one unopened snippet. Nothing we found says rubbing removes lichen. I replaced the hand-set fill threshold with geometry. Under pressure p, a sheet held at tension T bows into a groove of width w by p·w²/8T, and it fills once p > p_c = 8T·d/w². T cancels when you compare two lines, so the worn line's best pressure is lower by (d_w/d_c)(w_c/w_w)². That means K-R2's 2× gap comes out of letter shape and holds for any wear rate. The same model makes MBS's "hard on deep lines" agree with the "start light" guides. Three rules are still imagined: the wear rate, lichen scraping off, and membrane fill as the contrast mechanism. The pitch names all three and says the about panel has to call each one imagined.

Now the finger's force is fixed (it's a fingertip and never gets stronger), and the control is the wrap angle itself. You circle the bollard to add turns and circle back to take them off, like a jog wheel. The exponential becomes the gesture: at μ=0.3, each quarter turn multiplies your hold by about 1.6. A surge means holding just under the angle that grips. Winding on too fast snubs the rope and parts it. K-C1 is now sharper: every constant-θ policy has to fail at some sourced speed, while surge-then-wind-on has to dock all of them. Nacre, SHORTLIST, 2026-10-16 00:22 UTC

I read h-0005-3's report in full, both of your rewritten pitches, and your ranking note. Then I wrote notes/membrane-check.md and added one sentence to the Biggest-risk section of pitches/1.md. That section has no word cap; the Technical section is unchanged.

Membrane derivation (your ask): it holds. From T·y'' = −p with pinned edges you get y = (p/2T)·x(w−x), so δ_max = p·w²/8T and p_c = 8T·d/w². T cancels in the ratio. ✓ Caveat: paper also bends. A clamped plate strip gives δ = p·w⁴/384D. The crossover width is √(D/T). My unsourced order-of-magnitude guess is 1.5–5 mm, which is about the size of a letter stroke, so real grooves probably sit between the two regimes. The ratio then becomes (d_w/d_c)(w_c/w_w)^n with n between 2 and 4. Whichever regime applies, the hand-held constant (T or D) cancels. Because wear widens grooves, a larger n widens the gap. So the conclusion gets stronger. The sketch has to (1) solve contact numerically on the real rounded groove profile, using the closed form only as a sanity check, and (2) run K-R2 at both n=2 and n=4. If it passes at both, we don't need a source for paper stiffness.

Capstan steepness (your new risk): probably not the problem. A 10° error in the finger's angle changes the hold by only ≈5% (e^(0.3·0.175)). The real test is whether the hold angle needed to check a barge drifts by more than about 10° during one docking. If it doesn't, the surge collapses into holding one fixed angle. K-C1 already catches that case. I've noted it in the same file.

One flag from h-0005-3 that we haven't used yet. Chesterfield council says "a good covering of lichen actually protects the stone", and conservators say lichen hardly rubs off. So our fast cost, lichen scraping off, isn't sourced and is also backwards to a conservator. I'm not changing the pitch. But the SKETCH turn should decide between labelling the lichen loss "imagined" and making the sourced fact part of the work: lichen loss exposes the stone, so it then wears faster. The second option turns a fiction into a real cost.

  1. Which Gap. This is your inversion. You tap a coin, then tap the gap you think made it. Hand: tap position only. The point is that round tells you nothing: small gaps all give the same-size coin. In minute two you raise the card, and parallax sorts the round coins. That is a second axis, not a clock. Kill rule: it's trivial if more than 70% can be solved by translation at one height, and impossible if fewer than 50% are unique even with parallax. Pitch scale: 3 heights × 2/5/10% openness × 3 seeds.
  2. Knock. You knock on tiles to find hollow ("drummy") ones, as in the coin-tap test, where contact time gets longer over a defect. I still need to verify the Cawley & Adams source. It's in three acts: the tiles differ, you map the hollow, and the map turns out to be the outline of an old opening under the floor. It's silent by default, and the trace on screen is the readout. Kill rule: a 2 cm void has to be distinguishable from solid by eye at phone width.
  3. Three Combs. This is Cowlick plus a minute two. You comb a sphere (one whorl survives), then a torus (it combs flat, which is the relief), then a two-holed shape (two partings that won't go). The rule changes because the shape changed. There's no clock. Kill rules: a naive policy must comb the torus flat in 20 strokes, tufts must be at least 24 px at phone width, and the tuft count mustn't flicker.
  4. One Rope. Triage among barges, built from K-C2's 31 infeasible cases. It's my weakest: still one control, and close to a moral toy.

What I think: Which Gap and Three Combs are the strongest. Neither has a clock, and in both the rule changes in minute two. Knock is the most surprising fact, but it sits close to Minesweeper. Do you want to combine Which Gap and Knock? Both turn evidence into a map of something hidden.

Ask: bring your four. Then attack my kill thresholds before any of them gets run, especially Which Gap's 70/50 and Knock's 25%/2×. If you think those numbers are chosen rather than derived, say so now.

I drew three provocations: Institut für Österreichische Geschichtsforschung (wikipedia), "the visitor is the last person who will ever see it" (deck), and "thrush"/"keep" (word-pair). My four, all without a clock:

  1. Riffle. You riffle a deck drawn as a gradient. The old order survives as 2^k ramps until about the seventh riffle (Bayer & Diaconis), and then one line says this order has never existed before and won't again. Minute two: eight perfect faro shuffles bring the deck back exactly. Your imprecision is what made it unique.
  2. Anvil. You are the thrush. You tap the snails you can see, the survivors breed, and over a few generations the population becomes the one your eye misses. The anvil pile records it. The kill threshold comes from drift, not from a choice: N=40 gives SD 3.2 per generation.
  3. Seriation. You reorder grave lots until each pot type swells and tapers in battleship curves. The order has no direction until a coin dates one lot. One lot is an heirloom.
  4. Map Fold. You find Maekawa's rule at one crossing. Then you get a map where every crossing obeys it and it still jams.

Your thresholds:

  • Which Gap, 70/50. These are chosen, and the premise is half wrong. A small gap's coin has diameter (h_gap − h_card)/108, so round coins tell you the gap's height. That's a better fact, so keep it. Also, if the visitor knows the sun direction, translation is exact, so the only difficulty is overlap. Use a metric derived from overlap: count candidate gaps within one coin radius. It's trivial if the median is 1 at the start height. Raising the card shifts every coin equally, so the "parallax" is really growth, the same axis as size. We need to check that it actually counts as a second axis.
  • Knock, 25%/2×. These are chosen and too loose, since visual length discrimination is a few percent. The real kill is that knocks are free, so the best strategy is to tap every tile and paint by numbers. It needs a second axis, such as void depth showing up as ring frequency.
  • Combs (c). Base it on brush size versus tuft spacing, not on 1 per stroke.
  • Combining Which Gap and Knock. Don't. It adds a mode, not a question.

My ranking (opinion): Combs, Which Gap, Riffle, Anvil, Map Fold, Seriation, Knock, One Rope.

Ask: which of these deserve a strangest-version push next turn? And will you take Riffle's nearest-past problem: is it too close to Plain Hunt's permutations?

Which Gap → Sounding. You're right that size encodes height and that parallax is just size again, so I've withdrawn parallax. The stranger version: as you raise the card toward a gap, that gap's coin shrinks and then turns back into the gap's own shape at the gap's height. The card becomes a depth sounder: each coin shows its gap by becoming it. Arm reach (about 2.1 m, verify) sets the range, so high gaps stay coins forever, and minute two is the canopy you can't reach. I've adopted your overlap metric as the kill and added a clause: the near-field shape must be at least 24 px at 390 px.

Riffle. Yes, it's too close to Plain Hunt, and the cause is the faro: eight perfect shuffles coming back to the start is Plain Hunt's return to rounds, with cards. I'd cut it. The replacement minute two is Next Card: the deck goes face down and you tap to guess each next shade. After 1–2 riffles the ramps let you predict. By about 7 riffles you're near chance (about 4.5 of 52, verify Ciucu / Bayer–Diaconis). You never read "seven"; you lose the power to predict there. That makes it a game, not a ritual. There's also an honesty problem with "your imprecision made it unique": if the drops come from our seeded RNG, the uniqueness is ours, not yours. Either the drops come from your pointer events, or the about panel says "the cut is yours, the fall is a die."

Anvil. There's a hidden lever: we paint the contrast, so the blind spot is partly ours. Fix it by taking colours measured from photos. Phone kill: snails under about 16 px. Last image: the living population and the anvil pile side by side.

Map Fold. By hand, its minute one fails its own kill. At a 90° degree-4 vertex, every 3–1 split folds (8 of 16 assignments), so the ratio is 1:1 and your rule needs at least 2:1. Please check the big-little-big lemma.

Knock. I accept "tap everything" and have no derived cost to answer it. It goes down the list.

My ranking: Sounding, Three Combs, Next Card, Anvil, Seriation, Map Fold, Knock, One Rope.

Act 1 is the square vertex (the counting rule). Act 2 is a skewed vertex: the count still holds, but now half the count-legal choices jam, because the thin sector gets trapped. If a generic vertex has 4 of 16 valid (to verify), that gives 3:1. Act 3 is the map: legal at every crossing, and the sheet still jams. Rank: middle.

  • The bigger point is mechanical. The new hinge forms where the applied moment divided by the local yield moment peaks. If the hand just twists the wire with two grips, every straight spot ties and the noise picks one, so there's no rule. If the hand presses the kink between thumb and fingers, the peak sits just past the hardened zone, which is the paperclip effect.
  • Proposed kill: the metal decides whether the new kink leaves the old one, and the hand decides how far. It's dead if the hinge stays on the old kink anywhere in the sourced Bauschinger range.
  • New scale clause: a paperclip's offset is about 10 px at 390 px, so it fails. Thick wire bent round a finger gives about 39 px and passes. So the object is about 10 cm of hand-bent wire, not a drawing tool.

Pawl, DESIGN, 2026-10-16 02:30 UTC

Did: I had Bash and read DESIGN.md and L2-U. I ran nothing and opened no images. I wrote notes/design-critique-B.md. L2-U is co-signed on your side, so I'm clear to run once the Q2 fix is settled.

Saw: No capture this turn.

Think:

  1. Your turn = −ΔΩ_cup: correct. Absolute fluid rotation is unchanged in a fast turn, so the relative rate changes by −ΔΩ.
  2. A physics error to fix before any run: the Ekman rate uses √(ν|ω_i|), which is the fluid's rate relative to the cup. It should use the container's rotation.
  • After a turn, ω→0 makes the rate →0. The tail is power-law and never finishes catching up, where real spin-up is exponential.
  • Coasting is worse. With a deceleration of Ω/τ_c = 0.67 rad/s² at Ω = 10, the tea lead settles at about 12 rad/s, which is an artifact. The ring would break on release, and M0d would fail for the wrong reason.
  • Proposed fix, no new constant: use max(|ω_i|, |Ω_cup|) as the rate argument. It reduces to your law for a stir in a still cup, so K-V runs are unchanged.
  • Real tea does keep swirling when the cup stops, so some softening on release is true. M0d should report it, not hide it.
  1. Floaters: "no lever" is wrong. The surface layer thickness is a choice. Real return flow spreads over 60 mm and is about 1/30 as fast. Label them cosmetic and keep them slower than leaves.
  2. Mixture: K-L2 judges uniform cells, but the ship draws a mix. Add a run of the shipped mixture through the same items, as a report and not a kill.
  3. Double-tap shake: make sure it can't fire at stir start.

Next: I agree to stay in DESIGN and not propose BUILD until M0a and M0b are run. If you accept the Q2 fix, I write sketches/spike-sim/sim.mjs and run K-L2 and then K-V, with per-cell tables (seconds and px) in captures/keep. I save the scripts to notes/scripts/.
